    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... I mean, I know elevators aren't actually dangerous or anything, but whenever I have to get in one, I just get so scared, like I'm going to be trapped in there forever, and I just can't make myself feel normal about it. It’s so frustrating. Like, the other day at work—I work construction, mostly drywall and framing—we were on a site downtown, one of those old buildings they’re gutting. The service elevator was this ancient, creaky thing, and my foreman, Dave, he’s always rushing everyone. He’s a good guy, but man, he drinks way too much coffee. Anyway, he wanted me to take a load of materials up to the fourth floor, and I just stood there staring at the doors. I couldn't do it. I ended up carrying everything up the stairs, which took forever, and my knees are still killing me. I’ve been meaning to get new work boots, actually. The ones I have are worn right down on the heels, but they're so expensive, you know? But yeah, the elevator thing. It's just... I can't shake that feeling of being stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar feelings of fear or anxiety in situations other than being in elevators?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the elevators. I mean, I don't have a problem with small spaces in general. Like, I can crawl into a tight crawlspace to run some wire or check some framing, no problem. Actually, last week we were working on this residential job, a real nice house out in the suburbs, and I had to get right up under the eaves where the roof pitches down. It was so tight I could barely turn my shoulders, and there were these massive spider webs everywhere. I swear, one of the spiders was the size of a quarter. Dave, my foreman, he hates spiders. He won't go near them. It's kind of funny, actually, seeing this big guy get all jumpy over a bug. He made me go in there to do the work. But yeah, no, small spaces don't bother me at all, just the elevators. It's the doors closing, I think. But put me in a tight spot with a bunch of spiders, and I'm totally fine.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of being in the elevator that scares you, or is it more the idea of something happening while you're in the elevator, like it getting stuck?\n\nPatient: It's definitely the getting stuck part. Like, just thinking about the doors closing and then... nothing happens. The elevator doesn't move, and the doors won't open, and I'm just trapped in there. It's the thought of being stuck that really gets to me. It's like this one time, my cousin Jimmy—he's a mechanic over at that shop on 5th Street, the one with the big neon sign that's always flickering? Anyway, Jimmy got his hand stuck in an engine block once. He was trying to reach a bolt or something, and his hand just wedged in there tight. He said he panicked for a second, but then he just kind of wiggled it loose. He's always getting into scrapes like that. He's got these massive hands, too, like baseball mitts. I don't know how he does fine detail work with them. He fixed my transmission last year, actually, gave me a real good deal on it. But yeah, being stuck like that, not being able to get out, that's what freaks me out about the elevators.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for, I'd say, about 11 months now. Yeah, it started right around the time we lost our house and had to move into this apartment on the upper floor. It was a real mess, the whole housing thing. The bank just wasn't willing to work with us, and my husband—well, ex-husband now—he wasn't exactly bringing in a steady paycheck at the time. He was trying to start this landscaping business, but he bought all this expensive equipment, like these zero-turn mowers that cost more than my car, and then it rained for like three weeks straight in the spring, so he couldn't get any jobs done. He was always doing stuff like that, jumping into things without thinking. Like the time he decided we were going to breed iguanas in the spare bedroom. We had heat lamps everywhere, the electric bill was insane. Anyway, the apartment we're in now is on the sixth floor, and the building has this really old elevator. It smells like wet dog and cigarettes, and it always makes this loud clanking noise right before it stops. The landlord says he's going to fix it, but he says a lot of things. He's this guy named Sal, always wears these tracksuits, even in the middle of summer. But yeah, about 11 months, ever since we moved in there.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your ability to work or participate in other activities beyond just avoiding elevators?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still getting to work and going to my GED classes, so it's not like I'm totally shut down or anything. But it's... it's a hassle. Like, taking the stairs all the time, especially when I have my tools, it just wears me out before I even start my shift. And I've had to turn down a couple of assignments where the only way up was a construction elevator, which makes me look bad to Dave. Dave's the foreman I told you about, the one who hates spiders. He's actually been under a lot of pressure lately because his kid, the youngest one, just started playing travel hockey. Do you know how expensive that is? The gear alone is crazy, plus all the driving on weekends. He was telling me he had to drive all the way to Michigan last weekend, and his truck broke down on the highway. It was the alternator, I think. But yeah, taking the stairs and avoiding those elevator jobs, it makes me late sometimes, and I'm just less useful on some of the sites because I'm so worn out. I really want to get my GED though, so I can maybe get into a trade program, maybe plumbing. Plumbers make really good money, and they don't have to deal with elevators as much, mostly just basements and crawlspaces, which I'm fine with. But yeah, it definitely makes things harder at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having a panic attack or losing control in situations other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the elevators, honestly. I don't get worked up about other stuff. Like, I'm actually a pretty calm person most of the time. My friend Brenda, though, she panics about everything. We went to the grocery store the other day—the one over on 4th, with that really weird parking lot where the spots are way too narrow for normal cars, you know? Anyway, she completely lost her mind because they were out of her specific brand of almond milk. She drinks that vanilla unsweetened kind, which honestly tastes like wet cardboard if you ask me, but she swears by it. Says it helps her digestion or whatever. She's always doing these weird diets. Last month it was this cabbage soup thing, and her whole apartment smelled like a dirty gym sock for weeks. I couldn't even go over there to watch TV. But yeah, she was literally yelling at the stock boy about the milk, this poor kid who looked like he was maybe sixteen, tops. I had to practically drag her out of the aisle. So I guess I'm used to dealing with other people losing control, but me? No, I don't really get panicky like that unless I have to get in an elevator.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic attacks when you're in an elevator or thinking about being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... as soon as those doors start sliding shut, my chest gets really tight, and my hands get all sweaty. It's like all I can think is, \"I need out right now,\" even if we're only going up a couple of floors. It's just this instant reaction. Kind of like how my dog Buster acts when the vacuum comes out. Buster's a rescue, part beagle, part... I don't even know, maybe terrier? He's got these huge floppy ears but this wiry coat that sheds everywhere. You wouldn't think a dog that small could shed so much, but I'm constantly having to sweep. I bought one of those fancy pet hair vacuums off an infomercial late one night when I couldn't sleep. It was supposed to be ultra-quiet, but it sounds like a jet engine taking off in my living room. Buster absolutely hates it. He runs and hides under the sofa the second I open the hall closet. I tried giving him treats to calm him down, those little bacon-flavored ones, but he just ignores them. Anyway, yeah, when those elevator doors close, I get that tight chest and sweaty hands, and I just panic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack in a situation that didn't involve an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, it's just the elevators. I'm pretty chill otherwise. Even when things get stressful at work or whatever, I don't get that... that panic feeling. It's funny though, talking about stress, my sister just called me last night in a total state. She's trying to plan her wedding, right? And she decided she wants this rustic barn theme, which is super popular right now. But she didn't realize that barns don't usually have air conditioning, and she's getting married in August. So now she's freaking out about people sweating through their clothes, and she's looking into renting these massive industrial fans. But the fans are ugly, so she wants to decorate them with sunflowers or something? I told her, 'Tanya, nobody is going to care about the fans if they're melting,' but she wouldn't listen. She was going on and on for like an hour about the color scheme for the bridesmaid dresses—she wants this weird sage green that washes everybody out. I'm supposed to be a bridesmaid, and I already know I'm going to look terrible in it. But anyway, no, I don't have panic attacks about anything else. Just the elevators.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or fearful in any other enclosed spaces besides elevators?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Enclosed spaces are mostly fine, as long as it's not an elevator. Like, I don't mind small rooms or anything. I actually had to squeeze into this tiny crawlspace at work last week—I do construction, mostly framing and drywall, but sometimes we have to get into these really weird spots. Anyway, we were working on this old Victorian house over on Elm Street. Beautiful place, honestly, but the wiring was a total nightmare. The guy who owned it before must have done all the work himself because nothing was up to code. My boss, Frank, he's this big burly guy who can barely fit through a normal door, let alone a crawlspace, so he sent me in. It was super dusty, and I found this old glass bottle from like the 1920s just sitting there in the dirt. It had this faded label that said something about a \"miracle tonic.\" I actually brought it home and put it on my windowsill. I love finding old stuff like that on job sites. A couple months ago I found a stack of old newspapers from World War II under some floorboards. But yeah, being in that tiny crawlspace didn't bother me at all. It's just the elevators that get me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other situation where you might feel trapped, like in an elevator, and if so, does it scare you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't really get that trapped feeling anywhere else. Like, even if I'm actually stuck somewhere, it doesn't scare me. Take last Tuesday, I got stuck in this massive traffic jam on the highway. We were just sitting there parked on the asphalt for, I don't know, maybe two hours? Turns out a semi-truck had overturned and spilled a whole load of... I think it was gravel, or maybe sand? Anyway, it was a total mess. \n\nI was riding shotgun in my buddy Dave's truck, and his AC is completely busted. Dave's a good guy, works on the framing crew with me, but he refuses to take his truck to a real mechanic. He watches these DIY videos online and thinks he can fix everything himself. Half the time he just makes it worse, honestly. Like last month he tried to do his own brakes and almost rear-ended a garbage truck. He's always trying to save a buck, which I get, but come on. \n\nBut yeah, we were just baking in that hot cab for hours, couldn't open the doors because cars were right up next to us, totally stuck. I was sweating through my shirt and annoyed as hell, but I wasn't scared or panicking or anything. So, yeah, it's really just the elevators that do it to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear is of elevators themselves, or is it more about the feeling of being trapped that happens to occur in elevators?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the elevators themselves. Like, the box, the cables, all of it. If it was just about being trapped, I would have freaked out in Dave's truck, right? But I didn't. Elevators just... they just creep me out. I don't know who invented them, but they probably didn't have to haul drywall up five flights of stairs when they broke down. We had this job a couple years ago, this big apartment complex downtown, and the freight elevator was out of commission for three straight weeks. Three weeks! We were carrying sheetrock up the stairs all day. My knees were absolutely shot by the end of it. I had to start wearing these heavy-duty knee pads that made me look like I was gearing up for a roller derby. My friend Sarah actually does roller derby. She goes by the name \"Smash-Squatch\" because she's like six-foot-two and hits like a freight train. She broke her collarbone last season during a bout in Portland, and she still finished the jam. She's crazy. But anyway, yeah, I'm pretty sure it's just the elevators that I'm afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators related to the idea of being in a confined space with no control, or is it something specific about elevators that scares you?\n\nPatient: Um, I really don't think it's about control or confined spaces, honestly. I think I just... I don't know, I just don't have an answer for that. It's just elevators. They just get to me. It's funny you mention control, though, because my sister, she's a total control freak. She planned my parents' 40th anniversary party last month, and she literally color-coordinated the napkins with the frosting on the cake. The cake was from this little bakery down on 4th Street, the one with the green awning? They make these incredible lemon bars. I used to go there every morning when we were doing a framing job on the building next door. The owner, this sweet older lady named Maria, she'd always give me an extra one for free. She said I looked like I needed the calories with all the heavy lifting. Which, fair enough, framing is exhausting. We were using this new nail gun on that job, a pneumatic one, and it kept jamming. Frank was so mad, he threw it across the room and it dented the drywall we'd just put up. We had to patch it before the client saw. But yeah, I don't know what it is about elevators exactly. I just know I don't like them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have panic attacks when you're not thinking about or physically in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. If I'm not around an elevator, I'm completely fine. I don't just randomly start panicking out of nowhere. I'm usually pretty relaxed, honestly. Like yesterday, I was just hanging out on my porch after my shift, having a beer and watching my neighbor try to back his boat into his driveway. He is so bad at it, I swear. He's got this massive truck and this tiny little aluminum fishing boat, and he just keeps jackknifing it. \n\nI was sitting there for maybe forty-five minutes just watching him struggle. I almost went over to help him steer it in, but his wife came out and started yelling at him, and I definitely didn't want to get in the middle of that. They argue all the time about his fishing gear taking up the whole garage. He actually asked if he could store some of his tackle boxes in my shed, but my shed is already full of spare two-by-fours and my old dirt bike. I haven't ridden that thing since I was like twenty-five, it probably needs a whole new carburetor by now. I bought it off this guy who used to frame houses with me, but he moved out to Nevada to work on a solar farm. \n\nBut anyway... what was the question? Oh, right, the panic stuff. Yeah, no, I don't get like that unless I have to actually deal with an elevator. Otherwise I'm totally normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I actually had to squeeze into this tiny crawlspace at work last week—I do construction, mostly framing and drywall, but sometimes we have to get into these really weird spots. Anyway, we were working on this old Victorian house over on Elm Street. Beautiful place, honestly, but the wiring was a total nightmare. The guy who owned it before must have done all the work himself because nothing was up to code. My boss, Frank, he's this big burly guy who can barely fit through a normal door, let alone a crawlspace, so he sent me in. It was super dusty, and I found this old glass bottle from like the 1920s just sitting there in the dirt. It had this faded label that said something about a \"miracle tonic.\" I actually brought it home and put it on my windowsill. I love finding old stuff like that on job sites. A couple months ago I found a stack of old newspapers from World War II under some floorboards. But yeah, being in that tiny crawlspace didn't bother me at all. It's just the elevators that get me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other situation where you might feel trapped, like in an elevator, and if so, does it scare you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't really get that trapped feeling anywhere else. Like, even if I'm actually stuck somewhere, it doesn't scare me. Take last Tuesday, I got stuck in this massive traffic jam on the highway. We were just sitting there parked on the asphalt for, I don't know, maybe two hours? Turns out a semi-truck had overturned and spilled a whole load of... I think it was gravel, or maybe sand? Anyway, it was a total mess. \n\nI was riding shotgun in my buddy Dave's truck, and his AC is completely busted. Dave's a good guy, works on the framing crew with me, but he refuses to take his truck to a real mechanic. He watches these DIY videos online and thinks he can fix everything himself. Half the time he just makes it worse, honestly. Like last month he tried to do his own brakes and almost rear-ended a garbage truck. He's always trying to save a buck, which I get, but come on. \n\nBut yeah, we were just baking in that hot cab for hours, couldn't open the doors because cars were right up next to us, totally stuck. I was sweating through my shirt and annoyed as hell, but I wasn't scared or panicking or anything. So, yeah, it's really just the elevators that do it to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear is of elevators themselves, or is it more about the feeling of being trapped that happens to occur in elevators?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the elevators themselves. Like, the box, the cables, all of it. If it was just about being trapped, I would have freaked out in Dave's truck, right? But I didn't. Elevators just... they just creep me out. I don't know who invented them, but they probably didn't have to haul drywall up five flights of stairs when they broke down. We had this job a couple years ago, this big apartment complex downtown, and the freight elevator was out of commission for three straight weeks. Three weeks! We were carrying sheetrock up the stairs all day. My knees were absolutely shot by the end of it. I had to start wearing these heavy-duty knee pads that made me look like I was gearing up for a roller derby. My friend Sarah actually does roller derby. She goes by the name \"Smash-Squatch\" because she's like six-foot-two and hits like a freight train. She broke her collarbone last season during a bout in Portland, and she still finished the jam. She's crazy. But anyway, yeah, I'm pretty sure it's just the elevators that I'm afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators related to the idea of being in a confined space with no control, or is it something specific about elevators that scares you?\n\nPatient: Um, I really don't think it's about control or confined spaces, honestly. I think I just... I don't know, I just don't have an answer for that. It's just elevators. They just get to me. It's funny you mention control, though, because my sister, she's a total control freak. She planned my parents' 40th anniversary party last month, and she literally color-coordinated the napkins with the frosting on the cake. The cake was from this little bakery down on 4th Street, the one with the green awning? They make these incredible lemon bars. I used to go there every morning when we were doing a framing job on the building next door. The owner, this sweet older lady named Maria, she'd always give me an extra one for free. She said I looked like I needed the calories with all the heavy lifting. Which, fair enough, framing is exhausting. We were using this new nail gun on that job, a pneumatic one, and it kept jamming. Frank was so mad, he threw it across the room and it dented the drywall we'd just put up. We had to patch it before the client saw. But yeah, I don't know what it is about elevators exactly. I just know I don't like them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have panic attacks when you're not thinking about or physically in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. If I'm not around an elevator, I'm completely fine. I don't just randomly start panicking out of nowhere. I'm usually pretty relaxed, honestly. Like yesterday, I was just hanging out on my porch after my shift, having a beer and watching my neighbor try to back his boat into his driveway. He is so bad at it, I swear. He's got this massive truck and this tiny little aluminum fishing boat, and he just keeps jackknifing it. \n\nI was sitting there for maybe forty-five minutes just watching him struggle. I almost went over to help him steer it in, but his wife came out and started yelling at him, and I definitely didn't want to get in the middle of that. They argue all the time about his fishing gear taking up the whole garage. He actually asked if he could store some of his tackle boxes in my shed, but my shed is already full of spare two-by-fours and my old dirt bike. I haven't ridden that thing since I was like twenty-five, it probably needs a whole new carburetor by now. I bought it off this guy who used to frame houses with me, but he moved out to Nevada to work on a solar farm. \n\nBut anyway... what was the question? Oh, right, the panic stuff. Yeah, no, I don't get like that unless I have to actually deal with an elevator. Otherwise I'm totally normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I really don't. Enclosed spaces don't bother me at all. Like I said, it's just the elevators. I mean, I work in construction, right? Half the time I'm crawling around in crawlspaces or pulling wire through these tiny little utility closets that are barely big enough to turn around in. 

There was this one job we did last summer, remodeling this old Victorian house over on Elm Street. The basement was basically a dirt pit, and I had to squeeze between the foundation and the old boiler to check some support beams. It was so tight I had to take my tool belt off just to fit. And the spiders down there, my god. Huge, hairy ones. My foreman, Dave—he's the one with the truck I mentioned—he's terrified of spiders. A guy who's six-foot-four and bench presses like three hundred pounds, and he screams like a little girl if he sees a daddy longlegs. We found this massive wolf spider under some old floorboards, and Dave literally jumped onto a pile of lumber. He wouldn't come back down until I scooped it up in a coffee cup and threw it outside. 

I used that same coffee cup later for my actual coffee, which I probably shouldn't have done, but we were out of clean mugs in the trailer. The trailer coffee is always terrible anyway, it tastes like burnt tires. But yeah, no, I'm fine in tight spaces. It's literally just elevators that get to me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... I mean, I know elevators aren't actually dangerous or anything, but whenever I have to get in one, I just get so scared, like I'm going to be trapped in there forever, and I just can't make myself feel normal about it. It’s so frustrating. Like, the other day at work—I work construction, mostly drywall and framing—we were on a site downtown, one of those old buildings they’re gutting. The service elevator was this ancient, creaky thing, and my foreman, Dave, he’s always rushing everyone. He’s a good guy, but man, he drinks way too much coffee. Anyway, he wanted me to take a load of materials up to the fourth floor, and I just stood there staring at the doors. I couldn't do it. I ended up carrying everything up the stairs, which took forever, and my knees are still killing me. I’ve been meaning to get new work boots, actually. The ones I have are worn right down on the heels, but they're so expensive, you know? But yeah, the elevator thing. It's just... I can't shake that feeling of being stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar feelings of fear or anxiety in situations other than being in elevators?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the elevators. I mean, I don't have a problem with small spaces in general. Like, I can crawl into a tight crawlspace to run some wire or check some framing, no problem. Actually, last week we were working on this residential job, a real nice house out in the suburbs, and I had to get right up under the eaves where the roof pitches down. It was so tight I could barely turn my shoulders, and there were these massive spider webs everywhere. I swear, one of the spiders was the size of a quarter. Dave, my foreman, he hates spiders. He won't go near them. It's kind of funny, actually, seeing this big guy get all jumpy over a bug. He made me go in there to do the work. But yeah, no, small spaces don't bother me at all, just the elevators. It's the doors closing, I think. But put me in a tight spot with a bunch of spiders, and I'm totally fine.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of being in the elevator that scares you, or is it more the idea of something happening while you're in the elevator, like it getting stuck?\n\nPatient: It's definitely the getting stuck part. Like, just thinking about the doors closing and then... nothing happens. The elevator doesn't move, and the doors won't open, and I'm just trapped in there. It's the thought of being stuck that really gets to me. It's like this one time, my cousin Jimmy—he's a mechanic over at that shop on 5th Street, the one with the big neon sign that's always flickering? Anyway, Jimmy got his hand stuck in an engine block once. He was trying to reach a bolt or something, and his hand just wedged in there tight. He said he panicked for a second, but then he just kind of wiggled it loose. He's always getting into scrapes like that. He's got these massive hands, too, like baseball mitts. I don't know how he does fine detail work with them. He fixed my transmission last year, actually, gave me a real good deal on it. But yeah, being stuck like that, not being able to get out, that's what freaks me out about the elevators.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for, I'd say, about 11 months now. Yeah, it started right around the time we lost our house and had to move into this apartment on the upper floor. It was a real mess, the whole housing thing. The bank just wasn't willing to work with us, and my husband—well, ex-husband now—he wasn't exactly bringing in a steady paycheck at the time. He was trying to start this landscaping business, but he bought all this expensive equipment, like these zero-turn mowers that cost more than my car, and then it rained for like three weeks straight in the spring, so he couldn't get any jobs done. He was always doing stuff like that, jumping into things without thinking. Like the time he decided we were going to breed iguanas in the spare bedroom. We had heat lamps everywhere, the electric bill was insane. Anyway, the apartment we're in now is on the sixth floor, and the building has this really old elevator. It smells like wet dog and cigarettes, and it always makes this loud clanking noise right before it stops. The landlord says he's going to fix it, but he says a lot of things. He's this guy named Sal, always wears these tracksuits, even in the middle of summer. But yeah, about 11 months, ever since we moved in there.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your ability to work or participate in other activities beyond just avoiding elevators?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I'm still getting to work and going to my GED classes, so it's not like I'm totally shut down or anything. But it's... it's a hassle. Like, taking the stairs all the time, especially when I have my tools, it just wears me out before I even start my shift. And I've had to turn down a couple of assignments where the only way up was a construction elevator, which makes me look bad to Dave. Dave's the foreman I told you about, the one who hates spiders. He's actually been under a lot of pressure lately because his kid, the youngest one, just started playing travel hockey. Do you know how expensive that is? The gear alone is crazy, plus all the driving on weekends. He was telling me he had to drive all the way to Michigan last weekend, and his truck broke down on the highway. It was the alternator, I think. But yeah, taking the stairs and avoiding those elevator jobs, it makes me late sometimes, and I'm just less useful on some of the sites because I'm so worn out. I really want to get my GED though, so I can maybe get into a trade program, maybe plumbing. Plumbers make really good money, and they don't have to deal with elevators as much, mostly just basements and crawlspaces, which I'm fine with. But yeah, it definitely makes things harder at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having a panic attack or losing control in situations other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the elevators, honestly. I don't get worked up about other stuff. Like, I'm actually a pretty calm person most of the time. My friend Brenda, though, she panics about everything. We went to the grocery store the other day—the one over on 4th, with that really weird parking lot where the spots are way too narrow for normal cars, you know? Anyway, she completely lost her mind because they were out of her specific brand of almond milk. She drinks that vanilla unsweetened kind, which honestly tastes like wet cardboard if you ask me, but she swears by it. Says it helps her digestion or whatever. She's always doing these weird diets. Last month it was this cabbage soup thing, and her whole apartment smelled like a dirty gym sock for weeks. I couldn't even go over there to watch TV. But yeah, she was literally yelling at the stock boy about the milk, this poor kid who looked like he was maybe sixteen, tops. I had to practically drag her out of the aisle. So I guess I'm used to dealing with other people losing control, but me? No, I don't really get panicky like that unless I have to get in an elevator.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic attacks when you're in an elevator or thinking about being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... as soon as those doors start sliding shut, my chest gets really tight, and my hands get all sweaty. It's like all I can think is, \"I need out right now,\" even if we're only going up a couple of floors. It's just this instant reaction. Kind of like how my dog Buster acts when the vacuum comes out. Buster's a rescue, part beagle, part... I don't even know, maybe terrier? He's got these huge floppy ears but this wiry coat that sheds everywhere. You wouldn't think a dog that small could shed so much, but I'm constantly having to sweep. I bought one of those fancy pet hair vacuums off an infomercial late one night when I couldn't sleep. It was supposed to be ultra-quiet, but it sounds like a jet engine taking off in my living room. Buster absolutely hates it. He runs and hides under the sofa the second I open the hall closet. I tried giving him treats to calm him down, those little bacon-flavored ones, but he just ignores them. Anyway, yeah, when those elevator doors close, I get that tight chest and sweaty hands, and I just panic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack in a situation that didn't involve an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, it's just the elevators. I'm pretty chill otherwise. Even when things get stressful at work or whatever, I don't get that... that panic feeling. It's funny though, talking about stress, my sister just called me last night in a total state. She's trying to plan her wedding, right? And she decided she wants this rustic barn theme, which is super popular right now. But she didn't realize that barns don't usually have air conditioning, and she's getting married in August. So now she's freaking out about people sweating through their clothes, and she's looking into renting these massive industrial fans. But the fans are ugly, so she wants to decorate them with sunflowers or something? I told her, 'Tanya, nobody is going to care about the fans if they're melting,' but she wouldn't listen. She was going on and on for like an hour about the color scheme for the bridesmaid dresses—she wants this weird sage green that washes everybody out. I'm supposed to be a bridesmaid, and I already know I'm going to look terrible in it. But anyway, no, I don't have panic attacks about anything else. Just the elevators.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or fearful in any other enclosed spaces besides elevators?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Enclosed spaces are mostly fine, as long as it's not an elevator. Like, I don't mind small rooms or anything. I actually had to squeeze into this tiny crawlspace at work last week—I do construction, mostly framing and drywall, but sometimes we have to get into these really weird spots. Anyway, we were working on this old Victorian house over on Elm Street. Beautiful place, honestly, but the wiring was a total nightmare. The guy who owned it before must have done all the work himself because nothing was up to code. My boss, Frank, he's this big burly guy who can barely fit through a normal door, let alone a crawlspace, so he sent me in. It was super dusty, and I found this old glass bottle from like the 1920s just sitting there in the dirt. It had this faded label that said something about a \"miracle tonic.\" I actually brought it home and put it on my windowsill. I love finding old stuff like that on job sites. A couple months ago I found a stack of old newspapers from World War II under some floorboards. But yeah, being in that tiny crawlspace didn't bother me at all. It's just the elevators that get me.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other situation where you might feel trapped, like in an elevator, and if so, does it scare you?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't really get that trapped feeling anywhere else. Like, even if I'm actually stuck somewhere, it doesn't scare me. Take last Tuesday, I got stuck in this massive traffic jam on the highway. We were just sitting there parked on the asphalt for, I don't know, maybe two hours? Turns out a semi-truck had overturned and spilled a whole load of... I think it was gravel, or maybe sand? Anyway, it was a total mess. \n\nI was riding shotgun in my buddy Dave's truck, and his AC is completely busted. Dave's a good guy, works on the framing crew with me, but he refuses to take his truck to a real mechanic. He watches these DIY videos online and thinks he can fix everything himself. Half the time he just makes it worse, honestly. Like last month he tried to do his own brakes and almost rear-ended a garbage truck. He's always trying to save a buck, which I get, but come on. \n\nBut yeah, we were just baking in that hot cab for hours, couldn't open the doors because cars were right up next to us, totally stuck. I was sweating through my shirt and annoyed as hell, but I wasn't scared or panicking or anything. So, yeah, it's really just the elevators that do it to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear is of elevators themselves, or is it more about the feeling of being trapped that happens to occur in elevators?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the elevators themselves. Like, the box, the cables, all of it. If it was just about being trapped, I would have freaked out in Dave's truck, right? But I didn't. Elevators just... they just creep me out. I don't know who invented them, but they probably didn't have to haul drywall up five flights of stairs when they broke down. We had this job a couple years ago, this big apartment complex downtown, and the freight elevator was out of commission for three straight weeks. Three weeks! We were carrying sheetrock up the stairs all day. My knees were absolutely shot by the end of it. I had to start wearing these heavy-duty knee pads that made me look like I was gearing up for a roller derby. My friend Sarah actually does roller derby. She goes by the name \"Smash-Squatch\" because she's like six-foot-two and hits like a freight train. She broke her collarbone last season during a bout in Portland, and she still finished the jam. She's crazy. But anyway, yeah, I'm pretty sure it's just the elevators that I'm afraid of.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators related to the idea of being in a confined space with no control, or is it something specific about elevators that scares you?\n\nPatient: Um, I really don't think it's about control or confined spaces, honestly. I think I just... I don't know, I just don't have an answer for that. It's just elevators. They just get to me. It's funny you mention control, though, because my sister, she's a total control freak. She planned my parents' 40th anniversary party last month, and she literally color-coordinated the napkins with the frosting on the cake. The cake was from this little bakery down on 4th Street, the one with the green awning? They make these incredible lemon bars. I used to go there every morning when we were doing a framing job on the building next door. The owner, this sweet older lady named Maria, she'd always give me an extra one for free. She said I looked like I needed the calories with all the heavy lifting. Which, fair enough, framing is exhausting. We were using this new nail gun on that job, a pneumatic one, and it kept jamming. Frank was so mad, he threw it across the room and it dented the drywall we'd just put up. We had to patch it before the client saw. But yeah, I don't know what it is about elevators exactly. I just know I don't like them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have panic attacks when you're not thinking about or physically in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. If I'm not around an elevator, I'm completely fine. I don't just randomly start panicking out of nowhere. I'm usually pretty relaxed, honestly. Like yesterday, I was just hanging out on my porch after my shift, having a beer and watching my neighbor try to back his boat into his driveway. He is so bad at it, I swear. He's got this massive truck and this tiny little aluminum fishing boat, and he just keeps jackknifing it. \n\nI was sitting there for maybe forty-five minutes just watching him struggle. I almost went over to help him steer it in, but his wife came out and started yelling at him, and I definitely didn't want to get in the middle of that. They argue all the time about his fishing gear taking up the whole garage. He actually asked if he could store some of his tackle boxes in my shed, but my shed is already full of spare two-by-fours and my old dirt bike. I haven't ridden that thing since I was like twenty-five, it probably needs a whole new carburetor by now. I bought it off this guy who used to frame houses with me, but he moved out to Nevada to work on a solar farm. \n\nBut anyway... what was the question? Oh, right, the panic stuff. Yeah, no, I don't get like that unless I have to actually deal with an elevator. Otherwise I'm totally normal.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation, aside from elevators, where you feel a similar sense of fear or anxiety about being in an enclosed space?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Enclosed spaces don't bother me at all. Like I said, it's just the elevators. I mean, I work in construction, right? Half the time I'm crawling around in crawlspaces or pulling wire through these tiny little utility closets that are barely big enough to turn around in. \n\nThere was this one job we did last summer, remodeling this old Victorian house over on Elm Street. The basement was basically a dirt pit, and I had to squeeze between the foundation and the old boiler to check some support beams. It was so tight I had to take my tool belt off just to fit. And the spiders down there, my god. Huge, hairy ones. My foreman, Dave—he's the one with the truck I mentioned—he's terrified of spiders. A guy who's six-foot-four and bench presses like three hundred pounds, and he screams like a little girl if he sees a daddy longlegs. We found this massive wolf spider under some old floorboards, and Dave literally jumped onto a pile of lumber. He wouldn't come back down until I scooped it up in a coffee cup and threw it outside. \n\nI used that same coffee cup later for my actual coffee, which I probably shouldn't have done, but we were out of clean mugs in the trailer. The trailer coffee is always terrible anyway, it tastes like burnt tires. But yeah, no, I'm fine in tight spaces. It's literally just elevators that get to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess... I mean, I know they aren't actually dangerous. Like, intellectually, I know the cables aren't going to just snap and send me plummeting. But when I have to actually get in one, I just get this overwhelming fear that I'm going to be trapped in there. The doors will close and they just won't open again, and I can't make myself feel normal about it. It's that trapped feeling, for sure. 

Which is weird, because like I said, tight spaces don't bother me. But elevators... ugh. It reminds me of this time my cousin got stuck in an elevator in a hotel in Chicago. He was in there for like three hours. He said they had to call the fire department to pry the doors open. He was there for a convention, something to do with plumbing supplies? He sells those high-end faucets and showerheads to contractors. He makes a killing doing it, too. He drives this gorgeous restored '69 Mustang. Cherry red. He let me drive it once when we were at a family reunion at Lake Shelbyville. The clutch on that thing was so stiff, my leg was shaking by the time we got back to the cabin. We used to rent this big cabin every summer, the whole extended family. My aunt would make these huge vats of chili, and we'd sit around the fire pit... but yeah, I just get so scared of being trapped in the elevator. I can't shake it.

But yeah, being stuck like that, not being able to get out, that's what freaks me out about the elevators.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of elevators?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for, I'd say, about 11 months now. Yeah, it started right around the time we lost our house and had to move into this apartment on the upper floor. It was a real mess, the whole housing thing. The bank just wasn't willing to work with us, and my husband—well, ex-husband now—he wasn't exactly bringing in a steady paycheck at the time. He was trying to start this landscaping business, but he bought all this expensive equipment, like these zero-turn mowers that cost more than my car, and then it rained for like three weeks straight in the spring, so he couldn't get any jobs done. He was always doing stuff like that, jumping into things without thinking. Like the time he decided we were going to breed iguanas in the spare bedroom. We had heat lamps everywhere, the electric bill was insane. Anyway, the apartment we're in now is on the sixth floor, and the building has this really old elevator. It smells like wet dog and cigarettes, and it always makes this loud clanking noise right before it stops. The landlord says he's going to fix it, but he says a lot of things. He's this guy named Sal, always wears these tracksuits, even in the middle of summer. And I've had to turn down a couple of assignments where the only way up was a construction elevator, which makes me look bad to Dave. Dave's the foreman I told you about, the one who hates spiders. He's actually been under a lot of pressure lately because his kid, the youngest one, just started playing travel hockey. Do you know how expensive that is? The gear alone is crazy, plus all the driving on weekends. He was telling me he had to drive all the way to Michigan last weekend, and his truck broke down on the highway. It was the alternator, I think. But yeah, taking the stairs and avoiding those elevator jobs, it makes me late sometimes, and I'm just less useful on some of the sites because I'm so worn out. I really want to get my GED though, so I can maybe get into a trade program, maybe plumbing. Plumbers make really good money, and they don't have to deal with elevators as much, mostly just basements and crawlspaces, which I'm fine with. But yeah, it definitely makes things harder at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about having a panic attack or losing control in situations other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the elevators, honestly. I don't get worked up about other stuff. Like, I'm actually a pretty calm person most of the time. My friend Brenda, though, she panics about everything. We went to the grocery store the other day—the one over on 4th, with that really weird parking lot where the spots are way too narrow for normal cars, you know? Anyway, she completely lost her mind because they were out of her specific brand of almond milk. Last month it was this cabbage soup thing, and her whole apartment smelled like a dirty gym sock for weeks. I couldn't even go over there to watch TV. But yeah, she was literally yelling at the stock boy about the milk, this poor kid who looked like he was maybe sixteen, tops. I had to practically drag her out of the aisle. So I guess I'm used to dealing with other people losing control, but me? No, I don't really get panicky like that unless I have to get in an elevator.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic attacks when you're in an elevator or thinking about being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... as soon as those doors start sliding shut, my chest gets really tight, and my hands get all sweaty. Buster's a rescue, part beagle, part... I don't even know, maybe terrier? He's got these huge floppy ears but this wiry coat that sheds everywhere. You wouldn't think a dog that small could shed so much, but I'm constantly having to sweep. I bought one of those fancy pet hair vacuums off an infomercial late one night when I couldn't sleep. It was supposed to be ultra-quiet, but it sounds like a jet engine taking off in my living room. Buster absolutely hates it. He runs and hides under the sofa the second I open the hall closet. Anyway, yeah, when those elevator doors close, I get that tight chest and sweaty hands, and I just panic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a panic attack in a situation that didn't involve an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's funny though, talking about stress, my sister just called me last night in a total state. She's trying to plan her wedding, right? And she decided she wants this rustic barn theme, which is super popular right now. But she didn't realize that barns don't usually have air conditioning, and she's getting married in August. Just the elevators.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or fearful in any other enclosed spaces besides elevators?\n\nPatient: No, not really. We had this job a couple years ago, this big apartment complex downtown, and the freight elevator was out of commission for three straight weeks. Three weeks! We were carrying sheetrock up the stairs all day. My knees were absolutely shot by the end of it. I used to go there every morning when we were doing a framing job on the building next door. The owner, this sweet older lady named Maria, she'd always give me an extra one for free. She said I looked like I needed the calories with all the heavy lifting. Which, fair enough, framing is exhausting. We were using this new nail gun on that job, a pneumatic one, and it kept jamming. Frank was so mad, he threw it across the room and it dented the drywall we'd just put up. We had to patch it before the client saw. But yeah, I don't know what it is about elevators exactly. I just know I don't like them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have panic attacks when you're not thinking about or physically in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. If I'm not around an elevator, I'm completely fine. I don't just randomly start panicking out of nowhere. I'm usually pretty relaxed, honestly. Like yesterday, I was just hanging out on my porch after my shift, having a beer and watching my neighbor try to back his boat into his driveway. He is so bad at it, I swear. He's got this massive truck and this tiny little aluminum fishing boat, and he just keeps jackknifing it. \n\nI was sitting there for maybe forty-five minutes just watching him struggle. I almost went over to help him steer it in, but his wife came out and started yelling at him, and I definitely didn't want to get in the middle of that. They argue all the time about his fishing gear taking up the whole garage. He actually asked if he could store some of his tackle boxes in my shed, but my shed is already full of spare two-by-fours and my old dirt bike. I haven't ridden that thing since I was like twenty-five, it probably needs a whole new carburetor by now. I bought it off this guy who used to frame houses with me, but he moved out to Nevada to work on a solar farm. \n\nBut anyway... what was the question? Oh, right, the panic stuff. Yeah, no, I don't get like that unless I have to actually deal with an elevator. Otherwise I'm totally normal.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation, aside from elevators, where you feel a similar sense of fear or anxiety about being in an enclosed space?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Enclosed spaces don't bother me at all. Like I said, it's just the elevators. I mean, I work in construction, right? Half the time I'm crawling around in crawlspaces or pulling wire through these tiny little utility closets that are barely big enough to turn around in. \n\nThere was this one job we did last summer, remodeling this old Victorian house over on Elm Street. The basement was basically a dirt pit, and I had to squeeze between the foundation and the old boiler to check some support beams. It was so tight I had to take my tool belt off just to fit. And the spiders down there, my god. Huge, hairy ones. My foreman, Dave—he's the one with the truck I mentioned—he's terrified of spiders. A guy who's six-foot-four and bench presses like three hundred pounds, and he screams like a little girl if he sees a daddy longlegs. We found this massive wolf spider under some old floorboards, and Dave literally jumped onto a pile of lumber. He wouldn't come back down until I scooped it up in a coffee cup and threw it outside. \n\nI used that same coffee cup later for my actual coffee, which I probably shouldn't have done, but we were out of clean mugs in the trailer. The trailer coffee is always terrible anyway, it tastes like burnt tires. But yeah, no, I'm fine in tight spaces. It's literally just elevators that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators because of something specific about the elevator, like its appearance or the sound it makes, or is it because of the feeling of being trapped that can happen in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I mean, I know they aren't actually dangerous. Like, intellectually, I know the cables aren't going to just snap and send me plummeting. But when I have to actually get in one, I just get this overwhelming fear that I'm going to be trapped in there. The doors will close and they just won't open again, and I can't make myself feel normal about it. It's that trapped feeling, for sure. \n\nWhich is weird, because like I said, tight spaces don't bother me. But elevators... ugh. It reminds me of this time my cousin got stuck in an elevator in a hotel in Chicago. He was in there for like three hours. He said they had to call the fire department to pry the doors open. He was there for a convention, something to do with plumbing supplies? He sells those high-end faucets and showerheads to contractors. He makes a killing doing it, too. He drives this gorgeous restored '69 Mustang. Cherry red. He let me drive it once when we were at a family reunion at Lake Shelbyville. The clutch on that thing was so stiff, my leg was shaking by the time we got back to the cabin. We used to rent this big cabin every summer, the whole extended family. My aunt would make these huge vats of chili, and we'd sit around the fire pit... but yeah, I just get so scared of being trapped in the elevator. I can't shake it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't think it's the movement or the sounds, no. It's really just the being trapped part. The doors shutting and knowing I can't just walk out if I want to. Like, if I'm in a crawlspace at work, I can always just back out, you know? But in an elevator, you're just... stuck. Waiting. 

Speaking of waiting, I spent four hours at the DMV last week just to renew my registration. Four hours! The line was out the door. And the lady working the counter looked like she hated every single person who walked up to her. She had these huge, thick glasses and kept sighing really loudly every time she had to type something in. I swear, the keyboard she was using looked like it was from 1995. It was one of those big clunky beige ones. I don't know why they don't update their system. My buddy Greg works in IT for the school district, and he says the government uses the most outdated tech imaginable. He was trying to explain to me how their servers work, but honestly, it went right over my head. He's a smart guy, though. He built his own computer from scratch, ordered all the parts online. He even put these LED lights inside the case so it glows blue. 

Anyway, yeah, it's not the noises or anything. Just the feeling of being locked in there with no way out.

I can't shake it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything specific about the elevator, such as its movement or the sound it makes, that triggers your fear, or is it purely the idea of being in a confined space with no immediate way out?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's the movement or the sounds, no. It's really just the being trapped part. The doors shutting and knowing I can't just walk out if I want to. Like, if I'm in a crawlspace at work, I can always just back out, you know? But in an elevator, you're just... stuck. Waiting. \n\nSpeaking of waiting, I spent four hours at the DMV last week just to renew my registration. Four hours! The line was out the door. My buddy Greg works in IT for the school district, and he says the government uses the most outdated tech imaginable. He even put these LED lights inside the case so it glows blue. \n\nAnyway, yeah, it's not the noises or anything. Just the feeling of being locked in there with no way out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's literally just elevators that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators because of something specific about the elevator, like its appearance or the sound it makes, or is it because of the feeling of being trapped that can happen in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I mean, I know they aren't actually dangerous. Like, intellectually, I know the cables aren't going to just snap and send me plummeting. But when I have to actually get in one, I just get this overwhelming fear that I'm going to be trapped in there. The doors will close and they just won't open again, and I can't make myself feel normal about it. He was there for a convention, something to do with plumbing supplies? He sells those high-end faucets and showerheads to contractors. He makes a killing doing it, too. He drives this gorgeous restored '69 Mustang. Cherry red. He let me drive it once when we were at a family reunion at Lake Shelbyville. The clutch on that thing was so stiff, my leg was shaking by the time we got back to the cabin. We used to rent this big cabin every summer, the whole extended family. My aunt would make these huge vats of chili, and we'd sit around the fire pit... but yeah, I just get so scared of being trapped in the elevator. I can't shake it.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything specific about the elevator, such as its movement or the sound it makes, that triggers your fear, or is it purely the idea of being in a confined space with no immediate way out?\n\nPatient: I don't think it's the movement or the sounds, no. It's really just the being trapped part. The doors shutting and knowing I can't just walk out if I want to. Like, if I'm in a crawlspace at work, I can always just back out, you know? But in an elevator, you're just... stuck. Waiting. \n\nSpeaking of waiting, I spent four hours at the DMV last week just to renew my registration. Four hours! The line was out the door. And the lady working the counter looked like she hated every single person who walked up to her. She had these huge, thick glasses and kept sighing really loudly every time she had to type something in. I swear, the keyboard she was using looked like it was from 1995. It was one of those big clunky beige ones. I don't know why they don't update their system. My buddy Greg works in IT for the school district, and he says the government uses the most outdated tech imaginable. He was trying to explain to me how their servers work, but honestly, it went right over my head. He's a smart guy, though. He built his own computer from scratch, ordered all the parts online. He even put these LED lights inside the case so it glows blue. \n\nAnyway, yeah, it's not the noises or anything. Just the feeling of being locked in there with no way out.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of elevators the only thing that currently causes you significant anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Honestly, doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. Like I told you, it's just the elevators. I don't get stressed about anything else. I'm pretty laid back. 

It takes a lot to get under my skin. Although, I will say, the traffic on I-95 lately has been testing my patience. They've been doing this construction near the exit for my house, and they have it down to one lane. One lane! During rush hour! It's a nightmare. Who even puts that on their car? It made me think of my uncle Bob. He actually owned a tractor. He had this little farm out in Ohio, mostly just corn and a few chickens. We used to go visit him in the summers when I was a kid. He let me drive the tractor once, but I accidentally ran over his prize-winning pumpkin. He was so mad. He had been growing it for this county fair contest and he swore it was gonna win the blue ribbon. It was huge, like the size of a beanbag chair. 

But yeah, no, I don't have any other anxieties. Just the elevator thing.
